2001:db8::85a3:0:0:8a2e:370:7334
2001:db8::1:2:3::4
2001:db8:85a3::12345
2001:db8:85a3::g123
```

**Sample Output:**
```
valid
valid
valid
valid
valid
valid
valid
invalid
invalid
invalid
invalid
```

```python
import re

def is_valid_ipv6(ipv6_address):
    """
    Validates if the given string is a valid IPv6 address.

    :param ipv6_address: A string representing an IPv6 address.
    :return: True if the string is a valid IPv6 address, False otherwise.
    """
    ipv6_pattern = re.compile('''
        ^  # start of string
        (?:
            (?:  # non-capturing group for 7 groups of 4 hex digits separated by colons
                [0-9A-Fa-f]{1,4}:  # 1 to 4 hex digits followed by a colon
            ){0,7}  # zero to seven repetitions
            (?:  # non-capturing group for the final group of 4 hex digits
                [0-9A-Fa-f]{1,4}  # 1 to 4 hex digits
            )  # end of final group
            |  # OR
            (?:  # non-capturing group for IPv6 addresses with double colons
                (?:  # non-capturing group for 1 to 6 groups of 4 hex digits separated by colons
                    [0-9A-Fa-f]{1,4}:  # 1 to 4 hex digits followed by a colon
                ){0,1}  # zero or one repetition
                :  # double colon
                (?:  # non-capturing group for 0 to 5 groups of 4 hex digits separated by colons
                    [0-9A-Fa-f]{1,4}:  # 1 to 4 hex digits followed by a colon
                ){0,5}  # zero to five repetitions
                (?:  # non-capturing group for the final group of 4 hex digits
                    [0-9A-Fa-f]{1,4}  # 1 to 4 hex digits
                )  # end of final group
                |  # OR
                (?:  # non-capturing for IPv6 addresses ending in double colon
                    [0-9A-Fa-f]{1,4}:  # 1 to 4 hex digits followed by a colon
                ){1,7}  # one to seven repetitions
                :  # single colon at the end
            )  # end of IPv6 addresses with double colons
        )  # end of main non-capturing group
        $  # end of string
    ''', re.VERBOSE)
    return ipv6_pattern.match(ipv6_address) is not None

def test_ipv6_addresses(ipv6_addresses):
    """
    Tests a list of IPv6 addresses for validity.

    :param ipv6_addresses: A list of strings, each representing an IPv6 address.
    """
    for address in ipv6_addresses:
        print(f'{address}: {"valid" if is_valid_ipv6(address) else "invalid"}')

# Sample test cases
test_ipv6_addresses([
    '2001:0db8:85a3:0000:0000:8a2e:0370:7334',
    '2001:db8:85a3::8a2e:370:7334',
    '::',
    '2001:db8::',
    '2001::1',
    '2001:db8::8a2e:370:7334',
    '2001:db8:85a3:0:0:8a2e:370:7334',
    '2001:db8::85a3:0:0:8a2e:370:7334',
    '2001:db8::1:2:3::4',
    '2001:db8:85a3::12345',
    '2001:db8:85a3::g123'
])
```<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `BankAccount` class in Python that supports the following operations:

1. **Initialization**: Create a bank account with an optional initial balance. If no initial balance is provided, the account should start with a balance of 0.
2. **Deposit**: Add a specified amount of money to the account. The deposit amount must be positive; otherwise, raise a `ValueError` with the message "Deposit amount must be positive."
3. **Withdraw**: Remove a specified amount of money from the account. The withdrawal amount must be positive; otherwise, raise a `ValueError` with the message "Withdrawal amount must be positive." If the account balance is insufficient for the withdrawal, raise a `ValueError` with the message "Insufficient balance for the withdrawal."
4. **Check Balance**: Return the current balance of the account.

**Sample Input/Output**:
```python
account = BankAccount(100)
account.deposit(50)
print(account.check_balance())  # Output: 150
account.withdraw(30)
print(account.check_balance())  # Output: 120
account.withdraw(150)  # Raises ValueError: Insufficient balance for the withdrawal.
```

```python
class BankAccount:
    def __init__(self, initial_balance=0):
        """
        Initialize a bank account with an optional initial balance.
        
        :param initial_balance: The starting balance of the account (default is 0).
        """
        self.balance = initial_balance

    def deposit(self, amount):
        """
        Deposit a specified amount of money into the account.
        
        :param amount: The amount to deposit (must be positive).
        :raises ValueError: If the deposit amount is not positive.
        """
        if amount > 0:
            self.balance += amount
        else:
            raise ValueError("Deposit amount must be positive.")
    
    def withdraw(self, amount):
        """
        Withdraw a specified amount of money from the account.
        
        :param amount: The amount to withdraw (must be positive).
        :raises ValueError: If the withdrawal amount is not positive or if there is insufficient balance.
        """
        if amount > 0:
            if self.balance >= amount:
                self.balance -= amount
            else:
                raise ValueError("Insufficient balance for the withdrawal.")
        else:
            raise ValueError("Withdrawal amount must be positive.")
    
    def check_balance(self):
        """
        Check the current balance of the account.
        
        :return: The current balance of the account.
        """
        return self.balance
```<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a vending machine simulation in Python. The vending machine should support the following operations:

1. **Initialization**: The machine should start with a predefined set of products and their prices.
2. **Accept Payment**: Given a list of coin values (in cents), calculate the total amount of money inserted.
3. **Dispense Product**: Given a product name and the amount paid, check if the product is available and if the payment is sufficient. If so, dispense the product and return the change. Otherwise, return an appropriate message.
4. **Show Products**: Display the list of available products along with their prices.

**Input**:
- The `accept_payment` method will receive a list of integers representing coin values in cents.
- The `dispense_product` method will receive a string (product name) and a float (amount paid).

**Output**:
- The `accept_payment` method should return a float representing the total amount of money inserted.
- The `dispense_product` method should return a string indicating the result of the transaction (dispensed product with change or an error message).
- The `show_products` method should print the available products and their prices.

**Sample Input/Output**:
- Input: `coins_inserted = [25, 25, 25, 10, 10]`
- Input: `product = 'cola'`
- Output: `Dispensing cola. Change: $0.50`

```python
class VendingMachine:
    def __init__(self):
        """
        Initializes the vending machine with a set of products and their prices.
        """
        self.products = {
            'cola': 1.50,
            'chips': 1.25,
            'candy': 0.75
        }

    def accept_payment(self, coins):
        """
        Calculates the total amount of money inserted based on the coin values.
        
        :param coins: List of integers representing coin values in cents.
        :return: Total amount of money inserted as a float.
        """
        total = sum(coins) / 100  # Assuming the coins are in cents
        return total

    def dispense_product(self, product, amount_paid):
        """
        Dispenses the product if available and sufficient funds are provided.
        
        :param product: String representing the product name.
        :param amount_paid: Float representing the amount of money paid.
        :return: String indicating the result of the transaction.
        """
        if product not in self.products:
            return "Product not available."
        elif amount_paid < self.products[product]:
            return "Insufficient funds."
        else:
            change = amount_paid - self.products[product]
            return f"Dispensing {product}. Change: ${change:.2f}"

    def show_products(self):
        """
        Prints the available products and their prices.
        """
        print("Available products:")
        for product, price in self.products.items():
            print(f"{product}: ${price:.2f}")

# Example usage
vm = VendingMachine()
vm.show_products()

# Simulate inserting coins and buying a product
coins_inserted = [25, 25, 25, 10, 10]  # 1.00 + 0.50 + 0.50 = 2.00
amount_paid = vm.accept_payment(coins_inserted)
result = vm.dispense_product('cola', amount_paid)
print(result)
```<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `Matrix` class that supports basic matrix operations: addition, subtraction, and multiplication. The matrix should be initialized with a list of lists, where each inner list represents a row of the matrix. Your class should include the following methods:

- `add(self, other)`: Adds two matrices and returns the resulting matrix. Raises a `ValueError` if the matrices do not have the same dimensions.
- `subtract(self, other)`: Subtracts the second matrix from the first and returns the resulting matrix. Raises a `ValueError` if the matrices do not have the same dimensions.
- `multiply(self, other)`: Multiplies two matrices and returns the resulting matrix. Raises a `ValueError` if the number of columns in the first matrix is not equal to the number of rows in the second matrix.

Implement the `Matrix` class with the above specifications. Ensure that the class handles invalid inputs gracefully by raising appropriate exceptions.

**Sample Input:**
```python
matrix1 = Matrix([[1, 2, 3], [4, 5, 6]])
matrix2 = Matrix([[7, 8, 9], [10, 11, 12]])
result_add = matrix1.add(matrix2)
result_sub = matrix1.subtract(matrix2)
matrix3 = Matrix([[1, 2], [3, 4], [5, 6]])
result_mul = matrix1.multiply(matrix3)
```

**Sample Output:**
```
8 10 12
14 16 18

-6 -6 -6
-6 -6 -6

22 28
49 64
```

```python
class Matrix:
    def __init__(self, data):
        """
        Initializes the Matrix with a list of lists.
        :param data: List of lists representing the matrix rows.
        :raises ValueError: If all rows are not of the same length.
        """
        if not all(isinstance(row, list) and len(row) == len(data[0]) for row in data):
            raise ValueError("All rows must be lists of the same length.")
        self.data = data

    def add(self, other):
        """
        Adds two matrices.
        :param other: Another Matrix instance.
        :return: A new Matrix instance representing the sum.
        :raises ValueError: If matrices do not have the same dimensions.
        """
        if not isinstance(other, Matrix):
            raise ValueError("The operand must be an instance of Matrix.")
        if self.get_dimensions() != other.get_dimensions():
            raise ValueError("Matrices must have the same dimensions to be added.")
        
        result = [
            [self.data[i][j] + other.data[i][j] for j in range(len(self.data[0]))]
            for i in range(len(self.data))
        ]
        return Matrix(result)

    def subtract(self, other):
        """
        Subtracts one matrix from another.
        :param other: Another Matrix instance.
        :return: A new Matrix instance representing the difference.
        :raises ValueError: If matrices do not have the same dimensions.
        """
        if not isinstance(other, Matrix):
            raise ValueError("The operand must be an instance of Matrix.")
        if self.get_dimensions() != other.get_dimensions():
            raise ValueError("Matrices must have the same dimensions to be subtracted.")
        
        result = [
            [self.data[i][j] - other.data[i][j] for j in range(len(self.data[0]))]
            for i in range(len(self.data))
        ]
        return Matrix(result)

    def multiply(self, other):
        """
        Multiplies two matrices.
        :param other: Another Matrix instance.
        :return: A new Matrix instance representing the product.
        :raises ValueError: If the number of columns in the first matrix is not equal to the number of rows in the second matrix.
        """
        if not isinstance(other, Matrix):
            raise ValueError("The operand must be an instance of Matrix.")
        if len(self.data[0]) != len(other.data):
            raise ValueError("Number of columns in the first matrix must be equal to the number of rows in the second matrix for multiplication.")

        result = [
            [sum(self.data[i][k] * other.data[k][j] for k in range(len(other.data)))
             for j in range(len(other.data[0]))]
            for i in range(len(self.data))
        ]
        return Matrix(result)

    def get_dimensions(self):
        """
        Returns the dimensions of the matrix.
        :return: A tuple (rows, columns).
        """
        return len(self.data), len(self.data[0])

    def __str__(self):
        """
        Returns a string representation of the matrix.
        :return: A string with rows separated by newlines and elements separated by spaces.
        """
        return '\n'.join([' '.join(map(str, row)) for row in self.data])
```<#sep#>You are tasked with writing a Python script that processes a CSV file containing stock market data. The CSV file includes two columns: 'Date' and 'Close', representing the date and the closing price of the stock, respectively. Your script should prompt the user to input the filename and the number of days for calculating the moving average. The script should then compute and output the moving average for each date in the file.

**Input:**
- The user will provide the filename of the CSV file.
- The user will provide the number of days for the moving average calculation.

**Output:**
- For each date in the CSV file, output the date and the corresponding moving average of the closing prices over the specified number of days.

**Sample Input:**
```
Enter the stock data CSV file name: stock_data.csv
Enter the number of days for the moving average: 5
```

**Sample Output:**
```
         Date         MA
0  2021-01-01        NaN
1  2021-01-02        NaN
2  2021-01-03        NaN
3  2021-01-04        NaN
4  2021-01-05  150.000000
5  2021-01-06  152.000000
6  2021-01-07  154.000000
...
```

```python
import pandas as pd

def calculate_moving_average(file_name, days):
    """
    Calculate the moving average of stock closing prices over a specified number of days.

    Parameters:
    file_name (str): The name of the CSV file containing stock data.
    days (int): The number of days over which to calculate the moving average.

    Returns:
    None
    """
    try:
        stock_data = pd.read_csv(file_name)
    except FileNotFoundError:
        print("The file was not found. Please check the file name and try again.")
        return
    except pd.errors.EmptyDataError:
        print("The file is empty.")
        return
    except pd.errors.ParserError:
        print("There was an error parsing the file. Please ensure it is a valid CSV.")
        return

    if 'Date' not in stock_data.columns or 'Close' not in stock_data.columns:
        print("The CSV file must contain 'Date' and 'Close' columns.")
        return

    try:
        stock_data['Date'] = pd.to_datetime(stock_data['Date'])
        stock_data = stock_data.sort_values('Date')
        stock_data['MA'] = stock_data['Close'].rolling(window=days).mean()
    except Exception as e:
        print(f"An error occurred while processing the data: {e}")
        return

    print(stock_data[['Date', 'MA']])

def main():
    """
    Main function to prompt user input and calculate the moving average.
    """
    file_name = input("Enter the stock data CSV file name: ")
    try:
        days = int(input("Enter the number of days for the moving average: "))
        if days < 1:
            raise ValueError("Number of days must be at least 1.")
        calculate_moving_average(file_name, days)
    except ValueError as e:
        print(f"Invalid input: {e}")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `Bookstore` class that manages an inventory of books. The class should support the following operations:

- **Initialization**: Create an empty inventory.
- **Add Book**: Add a book to the inventory with its title, author, and price.
- **Remove Book**: Remove a book from the inventory by its title. If the book is not found, print an error message.
- **Get Inventory**: Return a list of all books in the inventory, where each book is represented as a dictionary with keys `'title'`, `'author'`, and `'price'`.

**Input:**
- A series of commands to manipulate the bookstore inventory. Each command is on a new line and can be one of the following:
  - `ADD <title> <author> <price>`: Add a book with the given title, author, and price.
  - `REMOVE <title>`: Remove a book with the given title.
  - `GET`: Print the current inventory.

**Output:**
- For each `GET` command, print the current inventory as a list of dictionaries.
- For each `REMOVE` command that fails to find the book, print an error message.

**Sample Input:**
```
ADD To Kill a Mockingbird Harper Lee 15.99
ADD 1984 George Orwell 9.99
GET
REMOVE 1984
GET
REMOVE Moby Dick
GET
```

**Sample Output:**
```
[{'title': 'To Kill a Mockingbird', 'author': 'Harper Lee', 'price': 15.99}, {'title': '1984', 'author': 'George Orwell', 'price': 9.99}]
[{'title': 'To Kill a Mockingbird', 'author': 'Harper Lee', 'price': 15.99}]
Error: Book with title 'Moby Dick' not found in the inventory.
[{'title': 'To Kill a Mockingbird', 'author': 'Harper Lee', 'price': 15.99}]
```

```python
class Bookstore:
    def __init__(self):
        """Initialize an empty inventory list to store book dictionaries."""
        self.inventory = []

    def add_book(self, title, author, price):
        """Add a book to the inventory as a dictionary."""
        self.inventory.append({'title': title, 'author': author, 'price': price})

    def remove_book(self, title):
        """Remove a book from the inventory by its title. Print an error message if the book is not found."""
        found = False
        for book in self.inventory:
            if book['title'] == title:
                self.inventory.remove(book)
                found = True
                break
        if not found:
            print(f"Error: Book with title '{title}' not found in the inventory.")

    def get_inventory(self):
        """Return a list of all books in the inventory."""
        return self.inventory

# Example usage of the Bookstore class
if __name__ == "__main__":
    my_bookstore = Bookstore()
    commands = [
        "ADD To Kill a Mockingbird Harper Lee 15.99",
        "ADD 1984 George Orwell 9.99",
        "GET",
        "REMOVE 1984",
        "GET",
        "REMOVE Moby Dick",
        "GET"
    ]
    
    for command in commands:
        parts = command.split()
        if parts[0] == "ADD":
            my_bookstore.add_book(parts[1], parts[2], float(parts[3]))
        elif parts[0] == "REMOVE":
            my_bookstore.remove_book(parts[1])
        elif parts[0] == "GET":
            print(my_bookstore.get_inventory())
```<#sep#>You are given a dataset of customer reviews for a product, where each review consists of a text field and a binary rating (0 for negative, 1 for positive). Your task is to implement a function that uses the Naive Bayes algorithm to classify new reviews as either positive or negative. The function should take a list of reviews as input and return a list of predictions, where each prediction is either 0 (negative) or 1 (positive). Use the Gaussian Naive Bayes implementation from the scikit-learn library.

**Input:**
- A list of training reviews `X_train` (strings).
- A list of training labels `y_train` (integers, 0 or 1).
- A list of new reviews `new_reviews` (strings).

**Output:**
- A list of predicted labels (integers, 0 or 1) for each new review.

**Sample Input:**
```python
X_train = ["I hate this product", "This is the worst I have ever bought", 
           "Absolutely love this!", "I'm very happy with my purchase"]
y_train = [0, 0, 1, 1]
new_reviews = ["I love this product", "Not good, very disappointing"]
```

**Sample Output:**
```python
[1, 0]
```

```python
from sklearn.feature_extraction.text import CountVectorizer
from sklearn.naive_bayes import GaussianNB
from sklearn.pipeline import make_pipeline
import numpy as np

def train_naive_bayes_classifier(X_train, y_train):
    """
    Trains a Naive Bayes classifier on the training data and returns the trained model.
    
    Args:
    X_train (list of str): List of training reviews.
    y_train (list of int): List of labels (0 for negative, 1 for positive).
    
    Returns:
    sklearn.pipeline.Pipeline: A pipeline object that includes CountVectorizer and GaussianNB.
    """
    pipeline = make_pipeline(CountVectorizer(), GaussianNB())
    pipeline.fit(X_train, y_train)
    return pipeline

def predict_review_sentiment(trained_model, new_reviews):
    """
    Classifies new reviews as either positive or negative.
    
    Args:
    trained_model (sklearn.pipeline.Pipeline): A trained model created by the train_naive_bayes_classifier function.
    new_reviews (list of str): List of reviews to be classified.
    
    Returns:
    list of int: List of predicted labels (0 for negative, 1 for positive) for each review.
    """
    predictions = trained_model.predict(new_reviews)
    return predictions

# Example usage
if __name__ == "__main__":
    X_train = ["I hate this product", "This is the worst I have ever bought", 
               "Absolutely love this!", "I'm very happy with my purchase"]
    y_train = [0, 0, 1, 1]
    new_reviews = ["I love this product", "Not good, very disappointing"]
    
    model = train_naive_bayes_classifier(X_train, y_train)
    predictions = predict_review_sentiment(model, new_reviews)
    
    print(predictions)
```<#sep#>Implement a Python class `Rectangle` with the following specifications:

- The class should have an initializer that accepts two parameters: `width` and `height`.
- Both `width` and `height` should be positive numbers. If a non-positive number is provided, raise a `ValueError`.
- Implement properties for `width` and `height` with appropriate validation.
- Implement a read-only property `area` that returns the area of the rectangle.
- Implement a read-only property `perimeter` that returns the perimeter of the rectangle.
- Implement a method `resize(factor)` that resizes the rectangle by multiplying both `width` and `height` by `factor`. If `factor` is non-positive, raise a `ValueError`.
- Implement a `__str__` method that returns a string representation of the rectangle in the format: `Rectangle(width=<width>, height=<height>)`.

**Sample Input/Output:**
```python
rect = Rectangle(3, 4)
print(rect)  # Output: Rectangle(width=3, height=4)
print(rect.area)  # Output: 12
print(rect.perimeter)  # Output: 14
rect.resize(2)
print(rect)  # Output: Rectangle(width=6, height=8)
```

```python
class Rectangle:
    def __init__(self, width, height):
        """
        Initializes a new Rectangle instance with the given width and height.
        
        :param width: The width of the rectangle (must be positive).
        :param height: The height of the rectangle (must be positive).
        :raises ValueError: If width or height is not positive.
        """
        self.width = width
        self.height = height

    @property
    def width(self):
        """
        Returns the width of the rectangle.
        
        :return: The width of the rectangle.
        """
        return self._width

    @width.setter
    def width(self, value):
        """
        Sets the width of the rectangle.
        
        :param value: The new width of the rectangle (must be positive).
        :raises ValueError: If the value is not positive.
        """
        if value <= 0:
            raise ValueError("Width must be a positive float")
        self._width = value

    @property
    def height(self):
        """
        Returns the height of the rectangle.
        
        :return: The height of the rectangle.
        """
        return self._height

    @height.setter
    def height(self, value):
        """
        Sets the height of the rectangle.
        
        :param value: The new height of the rectangle (must be positive).
        :raises ValueError: If the value is not positive.
        """
        if value <= 0:
            raise ValueError("Height must be a positive float")
        self._height = value

    @property
    def area(self):
        """
        Returns the area of the rectangle.
        
        :return: The area of the rectangle.
        """
        return self.width * self.height

    @property
    def perimeter(self):
        """
        Returns the perimeter of the rectangle.
        
        :return: The perimeter of the rectangle.
        """
        return 2 * (self.width + self.height)

    def resize(self, factor):
        """
        Resizes the rectangle by the given factor.
        
        :param factor: The factor by which to resize the rectangle (must be positive).
        :raises ValueError: If the factor is not positive.
        """
        if factor <= 0:
            raise ValueError("Factor must be a positive float")
        self.width *= factor
        self.height *= factor

    def __str__(self):
        """
        Returns a string representation of the rectangle.
        
        :return: A string representation of the rectangle.
        """
        return f"Rectangle(width={self.width}, height={self.height})"
```<#sep#>You are tasked with writing a Python script that processes a CSV file by normalizing its numerical columns. The script should read the CSV file, apply normalization to each numerical column, and then write the normalized data back to a new CSV file. Normalization is defined as subtracting the minimum value of the column and then dividing by the range (max - min). Your script should handle potential errors, such as the CSV file not existing or containing non-numeric data in a numeric column. The script should also include appropriate comments and docstrings.

**Input:**
- A CSV file path as a string.
- The CSV file may contain both numerical and non-numerical columns.
- The CSV file may be empty or contain parsing errors.

**Output:**
- A new CSV file with the same structure as the input file, but with numerical columns normalized.
- If an error occurs, print an appropriate error message.

**Sample Input:**
```
input.csv
id,name,age,score
1,Alice,25,88
2,Bob,30,92
3,Charlie,22,78
```

**Sample Output:**
```
output.csv
id,name,age,score
1,Alice,0.5,0.3333333333333333
2,Bob,1.0,1.0
3,Charlie,0.0,0.0
```

```python
import pandas as pd
import os

def read_csv_file(file_path):
    """Read CSV file from the given file path.

    Args:
        file_path (str): The path of the CSV file.

    Returns:
        pd.DataFrame: The data from the CSV file read into a DataFrame.

    Raises:
        FileNotFoundError: If the file does not exist.
        pd.errors.EmptyDataError: If the file is empty.
        pd.errors.ParserError: If there are issues parsing the file.
    """
    if not os.path.exists(file_path):
        raise FileNotFoundError(f"The file {file_path} does not exist.")
    
    try:
        data = pd.read_csv(file_path)
    except pd.errors.EmptyDataError:
        raise pd.errors.EmptyDataError(f"The file {file_path} is empty.")
    except pd.errors.ParserError as e:
        raise pd.errors.ParserError(f"Error parsing the file {file_path}: {e}")

    return data

def normalize_data(data):
    """Normalize the numerical columns of a given DataFrame.

    For each numerical column, subtract the column's minimum value and divide by the column's range (max - min).

    Args:
        data (pd.DataFrame): The input DataFrame with numerical columns to normalize.

    Returns:
        pd.DataFrame: The DataFrame with normalized numerical columns.

    Raises:
        ValueError: If no numeric columns are found in the DataFrame.
    """
    numeric_data = data.select_dtypes(include=[float, int])

    if numeric_data.empty:
        raise ValueError("No numeric columns found in the DataFrame for normalization.")

    normalized_data = (numeric_data - numeric_data.min()) / (numeric_data.max() - numeric_data.min())

    # Reattach non-numeric columns to the normalized data
    for col in data.select_dtypes(exclude=[float, int]):
        normalized_data[col] = data[col]

    return normalized_data

def save_normalized_file(normalized_data, file_path):
    """Write the normalized DataFrame back to a CSV file.

    Args:
        normalized_data (pd.DataFrame): The DataFrame with normalized data.
        file_path (str): The file path to save the normalized CSV file.
    """
    normalized_data.to_csv(file_path, index=False)

def main(input_file_path, output_file_path):
    """Main function to read a CSV file, normalize its numerical columns, and write the normalized data to a new CSV file.

    Args:
        input_file_path (str): Path of the input CSV file.
        output_file_path (str): Path of the output CSV file with normalized data.
    """
    try:
        # Read the input CSV file into a DataFrame
        data = read_csv_file(input_file_path)
        
        # Normalize the numerical columns of the DataFrame
        normalized_data = normalize_data(data)
        
        # Write the normalized DataFrame to a new CSV file
        save_normalized_file(normalized_data, output_file_path)
        print(f"Normalized data has been written to {output_file_path}.")
    except Exception as e:
        print(f"An error occurred: {e}")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    # Example usage
    main("input.csv", "output.csv")

The calculator should interact with the user by prompting for two numbers and an operator. The program should handle invalid inputs gracefully and continue to prompt the user until a valid operation is performed. The calculator should support the following operations:

- **Addition**: `+`
- **Subtraction**: `-`
- **Multiplication**: `*`
- **Division**: `/`

The program should output the result of the operation or an error message if the input is invalid. The calculator should continue to run until the user decides to stop.

**Sample Input:**
```
1
5
3
2
yes
3
4
2
no
```

**Sample Output:**
```
Select operation:
1.Add
2.Subtract
3.Multiply
4.Divide
Enter choice(1/2/3/4): 5
Invalid Input
Enter choice(1/2/3/4): 1
Enter first number: 5
Enter second number: 3
5.0 + 3.0 = 8.0
Let's do next calculation? (yes/no): yes
Enter choice(1/2/3/4): 2
Enter first number: 4
Enter second number: 2
4.0 - 2.0 = 2.0
Let's do next calculation? (yes/no): no
```

```python
def add(x, y):
    """Return the sum of x and y."""
    return x + y

def subtract(x, y):
    """Return the difference of x and y."""
    return x - y

def multiply(x, y):
    """Return the product of x and y."""
    return x * y

def divide(x, y):
    """Return the quotient of x and y, or an error message if y is zero."""
    if y == 0:
        return "Error! Division by zero."
    else:
        return x / y

def calculator():
    """Run a simple calculator that performs basic arithmetic operations."""
    print("Select operation:")
    print("1.Add")
    print("2.Subtract")
    print("3.Multiply")
    print("4.Divide")

    while True:
        choice = input("Enter choice(1/2/3/4): ")

        if choice in ['1', '2', '3', '4']:
            try:
                num1 = float(input("Enter first number: "))
                num2 = float(input("Enter second number: "))
            except ValueError:
                print("Invalid input! Please enter numeric values.")
                continue

            if choice == '1':
                print(f"{num1} + {num2} = {add(num1, num2)}")

            elif choice == '2':
                print(f"{num1} - {num2} = {subtract(num1, num2)}")

            elif choice == '3':
                print(f"{num1} * {num2} = {multiply(num1, num2)}")

            elif choice == '4':
                result = divide(num1, num2)
                print(f"{num1} / {num2} = {result}")

            next_calculation = input("Let's do next calculation? (yes/no): ")
            if next_calculation.lower() != 'yes':
                break
        else:
            print("Invalid Input")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    calculator()
```<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `Product` class in Python. The class should have the following attributes and methods:

- **Attributes:**
  - `name`: A string representing the name of the product.
  - `price`: A float representing the price of the product.
  - `quantity`: An integer representing the quantity of the product in stock.

- **Methods:**
  - `apply_discount(discount_percentage)`: A method that takes a discount percentage as an argument and reduces the product's price by the specified percentage. The discount percentage should be between 0 and 100, inclusive. If the discount percentage is outside this range, raise a `ValueError`.
  - `restock(amount)`: A method that takes a number of units as an argument and increases the product's quantity by that amount. The amount should be non-negative. If the amount is negative, raise a `ValueError`.
  - `sell(amount)`: A method that takes a number of units as an argument and decreases the product's quantity by that amount. The amount should be non-negative. If the amount is negative, raise a `ValueError`. If there are not enough units in stock to fulfill the sell request, raise a `ValueError`.

**Sample Input/Output:**
- Input: `product = Product("Laptop", 1000, 20)`
- After `product.apply_discount(10)`, `product.price` should be `900.0`.
- After `product.restock(5)`, `product.quantity` should be `25`.
- After `product.sell(10)`, `product.quantity` should be `15`.
- Calling `product.sell(16)` should raise a `ValueError` with the message `"Not enough stock to sell 16 units."`.

```python
class Product:
    def __init__(self, name, price, quantity):
        """
        Initialize a new Product instance.

        :param name: The name of the product.
        :param price: The price of the product.
        :param quantity: The quantity of the product in stock.
        """
        self.name = name
        self.price = price
        self.quantity = quantity

    def apply_discount(self, discount_percentage):
        """
        Apply a discount to the product's price.

        :param discount_percentage: The discount percentage to apply.
        :raises ValueError: If the discount percentage is not between 0 and 100.
        """
        if not (0 <= discount_percentage <= 100):
            raise ValueError("Discount percentage must be between 0 and 100.")
        self.price *= (1 - discount_percentage / 100)

    def restock(self, amount):
        """
        Increase the product's quantity in stock.

        :param amount: The number of units to restock.
        :raises ValueError: If the restock amount is negative.
        """
        if amount < 0:
            raise ValueError("Restock amount must be non-negative.")
        self.quantity += amount

    def sell(self, amount):
        """
        Decrease the product's quantity in stock.

        :param amount: The number of units to sell.
        :raises ValueError: If the sell amount is negative or if there are not enough units in stock.
        """
        if amount < 0:
            raise ValueError("Sell amount must be non-negative.")
        if self.quantity < amount:
            raise ValueError(f"Not enough stock to sell {amount} units.")
        self.quantity -= amount

# Example usage:
product = Product("Laptop", 1000, 20)
product.apply_discount(10)
product.restock(5)
product.sell(10)
try:
    product.sell(16)
except ValueError as e:
    print(e)
```<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `BankAccount` class in Python that simulates basic bank account operations. The class should support the following functionalities:

- **Initialization**: Create a bank account with an owner's name and an optional initial balance (default is 0).
- **Deposit**: Add a specified amount to the account balance. The deposit amount must be positive.
- **Withdraw**: Subtract a specified amount from the account balance if sufficient funds are available. The withdrawal amount must be positive.
- **Get Balance**: Return the current balance of the account.
- **String Representation**: Provide a string representation of the account for easy readability.
- **Debug Representation**: Provide an unambiguous string representation of the account for debugging purposes.

**Sample Input/Output**:
```python
account = BankAccount("John Doe", 100)
print(account)  # Output: BankAccount(owner=John Doe, balance=100)
account.deposit(50)
print(account.get_balance())  # Output: 150
account.withdraw(30)
print(account.get_balance())  # Output: 120
account.withdraw(200)  # Output: Insufficient funds.
account.deposit(-10)  # Output: Deposit amount must be positive.
account.withdraw(-50)  # Output: Withdrawal amount must be positive.
```

```python
class BankAccount:
    def __init__(self, owner, balance=0):
        """Initialize a new bank account with the given owner and initial balance."""
        self.owner = owner
        self.balance = balance

    def deposit(self, amount):
        """Add the specified amount to the account balance if the amount is positive."""
        if amount > 0:
            self.balance += amount
        else:
            print("Deposit amount must be positive.")

    def withdraw(self, amount):
        """Subtract the specified amount from the account balance if the amount is positive and sufficient funds exist."""
        if amount > 0:
            if self.balance >= amount:
                self.balance -= amount
            else:
                print("Insufficient funds.")
        else:
            print("Withdrawal amount must be positive.")

    def get_balance(self):
        """Return the current balance of the account."""
        return self.balance

    def __str__(self):
        """Return a string representation of the account."""
        return f"BankAccount(owner={self.owner}, balance={self.balance})"

    def __repr__(self):
        """Return an unambiguous string representation of the account for debugging."""
        return f"BankAccount('{self.owner}', {self.balance})"
```<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `Book` class that encapsulates the details of a book. The class should have the following attributes: `title`, `author`, `publisher`, `year`, `isbn`, and `genre`. Your implementation should include:

1. An initializer `__init__` that sets up the book's attributes.
2. A method `__str__` that returns a string representation of the book in the format: `"Title: {title}, Author: {author}, Publisher: {publisher}, Year: {year}, ISBN: {isbn}, Genre: {genre}"`.
3. A class method `from_string` that takes a string in the format specified above and returns a `Book` instance.

**Sample Input:**
```
"Title: The Great Gatsby, Author: F. Scott Fitzgerald, Publisher: Charles Scribner's Sons, Year: 1925, ISBN: 9780743273565, Genre: Novel"
```

**Sample Output:**
```
Title: The Great Gatsby, Author: F. Scott Fitzgerald, Publisher: Charles Scribner's Sons, Year: 1925, ISBN: 9780743273565, Genre: Novel
```

```python
class Book:
    def __init__(self, title, author, publisher, year, isbn, genre):
        """
        Initializes a new Book instance with the given attributes.

        :param title: The title of the book.
        :param author: The author of the book.
        :param publisher: The publisher of the book.
        :param year: The year the book was published.
        :param isbn: The ISBN of the book.
        :param genre: The genre of the book.
        """
        self.title = title
        self.author = author
        self.publisher = publisher
        self.year = year
        self.isbn = isbn
        self.genre = genre

    def __str__(self):
        """
        Returns a string representation of the book.

        :return: A string in the format "Title: {title}, Author: {author}, Publisher: {publisher}, Year: {year}, ISBN: {isbn}, Genre: {genre}".
        """
        return f"Title: {self.title}, Author: {self.author}, Publisher: {self.publisher}, Year: {self.year}, ISBN: {self.isbn}, Genre: {self.genre}"

    @classmethod
    def from_string(cls, book_str):
        """
        Creates a Book instance from a string representation.

        :param book_str: A string in the format "Title: {title}, Author: {author}, Publisher: {publisher}, Year: {year}, ISBN: {isbn}, Genre: {genre}".
        :return: A Book instance with the attributes parsed from the string.
        """
        attributes = book_str.split(', ')
        book_dict = {key: value for key, value in (attr.split(': ') for attr in attributes)}
        return cls(
            title=book_dict['Title'],
            author=book_dict['Author'],
            publisher=book_dict['Publisher'],
            year=int(book_dict['Year']),
            isbn=book_dict['ISBN'],
            genre=book_dict['Genre']
        )

- Initialize an employee with attributes: `name`, `employee_id`, `department`, `position`, and `salary`.
- Update the employee's department.
- Update the employee's position.

Your implementation should include a comprehensive docstring for each method.

emp.update_department("Product")
emp.update_position("Senior Software Engineer")

```python
class Employee:
    """
    A class to represent an employee.

    Attributes:
        name (str): The name of the employee.
        employee_id (str): The unique identifier for the employee.
        department (str): The department where the employee works.
        position (str): The job position of the employee.
        salary (float): The salary of the employee.
    """

    def __init__(self, name, employee_id, department, position, salary):
        """
        Initialize the employee with the provided data.

        Args:
            name (str): The name of the employee.
            employee_id (str): The unique identifier for the employee.
            department (str): The department where the employee works.
            position (str): The job position of the employee.
            salary (float): The salary of the employee.
        """
        self.name = name
        self.employee_id = employee_id
        self.department = department
        self.position = position
        self.salary = salary

    def update_department(self, department):
        """
        Update the employee's department.

        Args:
            department (str): The new department for the employee.
        """
        self.department = department

    def update_position(self, position):
        """
        Update the employee's position.

        Args:
            position (str): The new job position for the employee.
        """
        self.position = position

    def update_salary(self, salary):
        """
        Update the employee's salary.

        Args:
            salary (float): The new salary for the employee.
        """
        self.salary = salary

    def give_raise(self, percentage):
        """
        Increase the employee's salary by a specified percentage.

        Args:
            percentage (float): The percentage increase in salary.
        """
        self.salary += self.salary * (percentage / 100)
```<#sep#>You are given a CSV file named `sales_data.csv` containing sales data with the following columns: `Item` and `SalesAmount`. Your task is to write a Python script that performs the following operations:

1. Read the CSV file and load the sales data into a list of dictionaries.
2. Calculate the total sales amount.
3. Determine the highest and lowest sales amounts along with their corresponding items.
4. Sort the sales data by sales amount in descending order.
5. Write the sorted data to a new CSV file named `sorted_sales_data.csv`.
6. Print the total sales amount, the highest sale item with its amount, and the lowest sale item with its amount.

**Sample Input (sales_data.csv):**
```
Item,SalesAmount
Laptop,1200.50
Smartphone,800.00
Tablet,300.75
Monitor,150.00
```

**Sample Output:**
```
Total Sales: $2451.95
Highest Sale Item: Laptop with $1200.50
Lowest Sale Item: Monitor with $150.00
Sorted sales data written to sorted_sales_data.csv.
```

```python
import csv

def read_sales_data(input_file):
    """
    Reads sales data from a CSV file and returns a list of dictionaries.
    
    :param input_file: Path to the input CSV file.
    :return: List of dictionaries containing sales data.
    """
    sales_data = []
    with open(input_file, mode='r', newline='') as file:
        reader = csv.DictReader(file)
        for row in reader:
            sales_data.append({
                'Item': row['Item'],
                'SalesAmount': float(row['SalesAmount'])
            })
    return sales_data

def calculate_total_sales(sales_data):
    """
    Calculates the total sales amount from the sales data.
    
    :param sales_data: List of dictionaries containing sales data.
    :return: Total sales amount.
    """
    return sum(item['SalesAmount'] for item in sales_data)

def find_highest_and_lowest_sales(sales_data):
    """
    Finds the highest and lowest sales amounts and their corresponding items.
    
    :param sales_data: List of dictionaries containing sales data.
    :return: Tuple containing dictionaries for the highest and lowest sales.
    """
    highest_sale = max(sales_data, key=lambda x: x['SalesAmount'])
    lowest_sale = min(sales_data, key=lambda x: x['SalesAmount'])
    return highest_sale, lowest_sale

def sort_sales_data(sales_data):
    """
    Sorts the sales data by sales amount in descending order.
    
    :param sales_data: List of dictionaries containing sales data.
    :return: Sorted list of dictionaries.
    """
    return sorted(sales_data, key=lambda x: x['SalesAmount'], reverse=True)

def write_sorted_sales_data(sales_data, output_file):
    """
    Writes the sorted sales data to a new CSV file.
    
    :param sales_data: Sorted list of dictionaries containing sales data.
    :param output_file: Path to the output CSV file.
    """
    with open(output_file, mode='w', newline='') as file:
        fieldnames = ['Item', 'SalesAmount']
        writer = csv.DictWriter(file, fieldnames=fieldnames)
        writer.writeheader()
        for row in sales_data:
            writer.writerow(row)

def main():
    input_file = 'sales_data.csv'
    output_file = 'sorted_sales_data.csv'
    
    sales_data = read_sales_data(input_file)
    
    if not sales_data:
        print("No sales data found.")
        return
    
    total_sales = calculate_total_sales(sales_data)
    highest_sale, lowest_sale = find_highest_and_lowest_sales(sales_data)
    sorted_sales_data = sort_sales_data(sales_data)
    
    write_sorted_sales_data(sorted_sales_data, output_file)
    
    print(f"Total Sales: ${total_sales:.2f}")
    print(f"Highest Sale Item: {highest_sale['Item']} with ${highest_sale['SalesAmount']:.2f}")
    print(f"Lowest Sale Item: {lowest_sale['Item']} with ${lowest_sale['SalesAmount']:.2f}")
    print(f"Sorted sales data written to {output_file}.")

if __name__ == '__main__':
    main()
```<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `Book` class that models a book with various attributes and behaviors. The class should support the following functionalities:

- **Attributes:**
  - `title`: A string representing the title of the book.
  - `author`: A string representing the author of the book.
  - `published_year`: An integer representing the year the book was published.
  - `genres`: A list of strings representing the genres of the book.
  - `pages`: An integer representing the number of pages in the book.
  - `isbn`: A string representing the ISBN of the book.

- **Methods:**
  - `__init__(self, title, author, published_year, genres=None, pages=None, isbn=None)`: Initializes a new book instance with the given attributes.
  - `describe_book(self)`: Returns a string describing the book, including its title, author, published year, and genres.
  - `add_genre(self, genre)`: Adds a genre to the book's list of genres if it is not already present.
  - `remove_genre(self, genre)`: Removes a genre from the book's list of genres if it exists.
  - `is_classic(self)`: Returns `True` if the book was published more than 100 years ago, otherwise returns `False`.

**Sample Input/Output:**
```python
book = Book("1984", "George Orwell", 1949, ["Dystopian", "Political Fiction"])
print(book.describe_book())  # Output: "Title: 1984, Author: George Orwell, Published Year: 1949, Genres: Dystopian, Political Fiction"
book.add_genre("Science Fiction")
print(book.describe_book())  # Output: "Title: 1984, Author: George Orwell, Published Year: 1949, Genres: Dystopian, Political Fiction, Science Fiction"
book.remove_genre("Dystopian")
print(book.describe_book())  # Output: "Title: 1984, Author: George Orwell, Published Year: 1949, Genres: Political Fiction, Science Fiction"
print(book.is_classic())     # Output: True
```

```python
class Book:
    def __init__(self, title, author, published_year, genres=None, pages=None, isbn=None):
        """
        Initializes a new book instance with the given attributes.
        
        :param title: The title of the book.
        :param author: The author of the book.
        :param published_year: The year the book was published.
        :param genres: A list of genres of the book.
        :param pages: The number of pages in the book.
        :param isbn: The ISBN of the book.
        """
        self.title = title
        self.author = author
        self.published_year = published_year
        self.genres = genres if genres is not None else []
        self.pages = pages
        self.isbn = isbn

    def describe_book(self):
        """
        Returns a string describing the book, including its title, author, published year, and genres.
        
        :return: A string describing the book.
        """
        genres_str = ', '.join(self.genres) if self.genres else "None"
        return (f"Title: {self.title}, Author: {self.author}, Published Year: {self.published_year}, "
                f"Genres: {genres_str}")

    def add_genre(self, genre):
        """
        Adds a genre to the book's list of genres if it is not already present.
        
        :param genre: The genre to add.
        """
        if genre not in self.genres:
            self.genres.append(genre)

    def remove_genre(self, genre):
        """
        Removes a genre from the book's list of genres if it exists.
        
        :param genre: The genre to remove.
        """
        if genre in self.genres:
            self.genres.remove(genre)

    def is_classic(self):
        """
        Returns True if the book was published more than 100 years ago, otherwise returns False.
        
        :return: A boolean indicating if the book is a classic.
        """
        current_year = 2023
        return current_year - self.published_year > 100
```<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a function `convert_units(value, from_unit, to_unit)` that performs unit conversions for length, weight, and temperature. The function should handle the following conversions:

- **Length**: millimeters (mm), centimeters (cm), meters (m), kilometers (km)
- **Weight**: milligrams (mg), grams (g), kilograms (kg), metric tons (t)
- **Temperature**: Celsius (°C), Fahrenheit (°F), Kelvin (K)

Your function should return the converted value as a float. If the units provided are not supported or do not match the same category (e.g., converting from a length unit to a weight unit), the function should raise a `ValueError`.

**Sample Input and Output:**
- `convert_units(100, "cm", "m")` should return `1.0`
- `convert_units(32, "°F", "°C")` should return `0.0`
- `convert_units(1000, "mg", "g")` should return `1.0`
- `convert_units(273.15, "K", "°C")` should return `0.0`

```python
def convert_units(value, from_unit, to_unit):
    """
    Convert a given value from one unit of measurement to another.
    
    Parameters:
    value (float): The value to be converted.
    from_unit (str): The unit of the input value.
    to_unit (str): The target unit for conversion.
    
    Returns:
    float: The converted value.
    
    Raises:
    ValueError: If the units are unsupported or do not match the same category.
    """
    # Define conversion factors in dictionaries
    length_factors = {
        "mm": 0.001,
        "cm": 0.01,
        "m": 1,
        "km": 1000
    }
    
    weight_factors = {
        "mg": 0.000001,
        "g": 0.001,
        "kg": 1,
        "t": 1000
    }
    
    # Temperature conversion functions
    def celsius_to_fahrenheit(c):
        return (c * 9/5) + 32
    
    def celsius_to_kelvin(c):
        return c + 273.15
    
    def fahrenheit_to_celsius(f):
        return (f - 32) * 5/9
    
    def fahrenheit_to_kelvin(f):
        return celsius_to_kelvin(fahrenheit_to_celsius(f))
    
    def kelvin_to_celsius(k):
        return k - 273.15
    
    def kelvin_to_fahrenheit(k):
        return celsius_to_fahrenheit(kelvin_to_celsius(k))
    
    # Determine type of conversion
    if from_unit in length_factors and to_unit in length_factors:
        meters = value * length_factors[from_unit]
        result = meters / length_factors[to_unit]
        return result
    
    elif from_unit in weight_factors and to_unit in weight_factors:
        kg = value * weight_factors[from_unit]
        result = kg / weight_factors[to_unit]
        return result
    
    elif (from_unit in {"°C", "°F", "K"}) and (to_unit in {"°C", "°F", "K"}):
        if from_unit == "°C":
            if to_unit == "°F":
                return celsius_to_fahrenheit(value)
            elif to_unit == "K":
                return celsius_to_kelvin(value)
            else:
                return value
        elif from_unit == "°F":
            if to_unit == "°C":
                return fahrenheit_to_celsius(value)
            elif to_unit == "K":
                return fahrenheit_to_kelvin(value)
            else:
                return value
        elif from_unit == "K":
            if to_unit == "°C":
                return kelvin_to_celsius(value)
            elif to_unit == "°F":
                return kelvin_to_fahrenheit(value)
            else:
                return value
    else:
        raise ValueError("Unsupported units or unit types")
```<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a simple text editor that supports basic operations: adding lines, inserting lines at a specific position, deleting lines, and displaying the current document. The text editor should handle commands in the following format:

- `ADD: <TEXT>`: Adds a new line of text to the end of the document.
- `INSERT <INDEX>: <TEXT>`: Inserts a new line of text at the specified index. If the index is out of bounds, the command should be ignored.
- `DELETE <INDEX>`: Deletes the line at the specified index. If the index is out of bounds, the command should be ignored.
- `DISPLAY`: Prints the current state of the document, with each line prefixed by its index.
- `EXIT`: Exits the text editor.

**Input:**
- A series of commands, one per line, until the `EXIT` command is encountered.

**Output:**
- For each `ADD`, `INSERT`, and `DELETE` command, print a confirmation message.
- For the `DISPLAY` command, print the current document with line indices.
- For invalid commands, print "Invalid command."

**Sample Input:**
```
ADD: Hello
ADD: World
INSERT 1: There
DISPLAY
DELETE 2
DISPLAY
EXIT
```

**Sample Output:**
```
Line added.
Line added.
Line inserted at position 1.
Current document:
0: Hello
1: There
2: World
Line deleted from position 2.
Current document:
0: Hello
1: There
Exiting the text editor.
```

```python
def text_editor():
    """
    Simulates a simple text editor that supports adding, inserting, deleting, and displaying lines of text.
    """
    document = []

    print("Welcome to the simple text editor!")
    print("Supported commands: ADD, INSERT, DELETE, DISPLAY.")
    print("Format: <COMMAND> [INDEX]: <TEXT> (INDEX is required for INSERT and DELETE)")

    while True:
        command = input("Enter command: ").strip()

        if command.startswith("ADD:"):
            line = command[4:].strip()
            document.append(line)
            print("Line added.")

        elif command.startswith("INSERT "):
            try:
                colon_index = command.find(':')
                index = int(command[7:colon_index-1])
                line = command[colon_index + 1:].strip()
                
                if 0 <= index <= len(document):
                    document.insert(index, line)
                    print(f"Line inserted at position {index}.")
                else:
                    print("Invalid index.")
            except ValueError:
                print("Invalid command format.")

        elif command.startswith("DELETE "):
            try:
                index = int(command[7:].strip())

                if 0 <= index < len(document):
                    del document[index]
                    print(f"Line deleted from position {index}.")
                else:
                    print("Invalid index.")
            except ValueError:
                print("Invalid command format.")

        elif command == "DISPLAY":
            print("Current document:")
            for i, line in enumerate(document):
                print(f"{i}: {line}")
        
        elif command == "EXIT":
            print("Exiting the text editor.")
            break
        
        else:
            print("Invalid command.")
            
if __name__ == "__main__":
    text_editor()
```<#sep#>You are given a dataset of sales records for a company's products. Each record contains the following fields: `product_id`, `quantity`, `price`, and `date`. Your task is to implement a function `calculate_sales_summary(sales)` that processes this dataset and returns a dictionary. The dictionary should contain the total sales amount and the average quantity sold for each product. The total sales amount is computed by multiplying the quantity and price for each sale. The average quantity sold is determined by dividing the total quantity sold by the number of sales for each product.

**Input:**
- A list of dictionaries, where each dictionary represents a sale record with keys `product_id`, `quantity`, `price`, and `date`.

**Output:**
- A dictionary where each key is a `product_id` and the value is another dictionary containing:
  - `total_sales`: The total sales amount for the product.
  - `average_quantity`: The average quantity sold for the product.

**Sample Input:**
```python
[
    {'product_id': 'p1', 'quantity': 2, 'price': 15.99, 'date': '2023-01-01'},
    {'product_id': 'p2', 'quantity': 1, 'price': 9.99, 'date': '2023-01-01'},
    {'product_id': 'p1', 'quantity': 3, 'price': 15.99, 'date': '2023-01-02'},
    {'product_id': 'p2', 'quantity': 2, 'price': 19.99, 'date': '2023-01-02'}
]
```

**Sample Output:**
```python
{
    'p1': {'total_sales': 71.94, 'average_quantity': 2.5},
    'p2': {'total_sales': 49.97, 'average_quantity': 1.5}
}
```

```python
def calculate_sales_summary(sales):
    """
    Processes a list of sales records and returns a summary dictionary containing
    the total sales amount and the average quantity sold for each product.

    :param sales: List of dictionaries, each containing 'product_id', 'quantity', 'price', and 'date'.
    :return: Dictionary with product_id as keys and a nested dictionary with 'total_sales' and 'average_quantity' as values.
    """
    summary = {}
    
    for sale in sales:
        product_id = sale['product_id']
        quantity = sale['quantity']
        price = sale['price']
        total_sales = quantity * price
        
        if product_id not in summary:
            summary[product_id] = {'total_sales': 0, 'total_quantity': 0, 'count': 0}
        
        summary[product_id]['total_sales'] += total_sales
        summary[product_id]['total_quantity'] += quantity
        summary[product_id]['count'] += 1

    for product_id in summary:
        summary[product_id]['average_quantity'] = summary[product_id]['total_quantity'] / summary[product_id]['count']
        del summary[product_id]['total_quantity']
        del summary[product_id]['count']

    return summary

# Test the function with the provided sales data
sales = [
    {'product_id': 'p1', 'quantity': 2, 'price': 15.99, 'date': '2023-01-01'},
    {'product_id': 'p2', 'quantity': 1, 'price': 9.99, 'date': '2023-01-01'},
    {'product_id': 'p1', 'quantity': 3, 'price': 15.99, 'date': '2023-01-02'},
    {'product_id': 'p2', 'quantity': 2, 'price': 19.99, 'date': '2023-01-02'}
]

print(calculate_sales_summary(sales))

1. **Introduction**: A brief introduction stating the purpose of the document.
2. **Data Summary**: Summary of the dataset including the number of rows, number of columns, and data types of each column.
3. **Statistics**: Summary statistics for each numeric column.
4. **Visualization**: A bar chart visualizing the distribution of values in a specified column. The chart should be saved as a PNG file and referenced in the Markdown report.

Your program should be able to handle any JSON file with a structure similar to the sample input provided below.

**Sample Input (data.json):**
```json
[
    {"Name": "Alice", "Age": 25, "Salary": 50000},
    {"Name": "Bob", "Age": 30, "Salary": 60000},
    {"Name": "Charlie", "Age": 35, "Salary": 70000}
]
```

**Sample Output (report.md):**
```markdown
# Data Report

## Introduction
This document provides a summary and analysis of the dataset.

## Data Summary
Number of Rows: 3
Number of Columns: 3
Data Types of Each Column:
Name      object
Age          int64
Salary       int64
dtype: object

## Statistics
Summary Statistics for Each Numeric Column:
|       |   Age |   Salary |
|-------|-------:|---------:|
| count |     3 |        3 |
| mean  |    26.7|    60000 |
| std   |     5.8|    10000 |
| min   |    25 |    50000 |
| 25%   |    27.5|    55000 |
| 50%   |    30 |    60000 |
| 75%   |    32.5|    65000 |
| max   |    35 |    70000 |

## Visualization
Bar Chart of the Distribution of Values in the 'Age' Column:

![Bar Chart](Age_bar_chart.png)
```

```python
import json
import pandas as pd
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt

def generate_markdown_report(json_file, visualization_column='Age'):
    """
    Generates a Markdown report from a JSON file.

    Parameters:
    json_file (str): Path to the JSON file.
    visualization_column (str): Column to visualize in the bar chart.

    Returns:
    str: Markdown formatted report.
    """
    with open(json_file, 'r') as file:
        data = json.load(file)

    df = pd.DataFrame(data)

    report = "# Data Report\n\n"

    report += "## Introduction\n"
    report += "This document provides a summary and analysis of the dataset.\n\n"

    report += "## Data Summary\n"
    report += f"Number of Rows: {len(df)}\n"
    report += f"Number of Columns: {len(df.columns)}\n\n"
    report += "Data Types of Each Column:\n"
    report += str(df.dtypes) + "\n\n"

    report += "## Statistics\n"
    report += "Summary Statistics for Each Numeric Column:\n"
    report += df.describe().to_markdown() + "\n\n"

    report += "## Visualization\n"
    report += f"Bar Chart of the Distribution of Values in the '{visualization_column}' Column:\n"
    report += "\n![Bar Chart]({column}_bar_chart.png)\n\n".format(column=visualization_column)

    plt.figure(figsize=(8, 6))
    df[visualization_column].value_counts().plot(kind='bar')
    plt.title(f"Distribution of {visualization_column}")
    plt.xlabel(visualization_column)
    plt.ylabel('Count')
    plt.xticks(rotation=45, ha='right')
    plt.tight_layout()
    plt.savefig(f"{visualization_column}_bar_chart.png")
    plt.close()

    return report
```<#sep#>You are given a dataset representing employees in a company. The dataset is structured as a list of dictionaries, where each dictionary contains the following keys: `name`, `department`, `salary`, and `hire_date`. Your task is to implement a function `get_top_paid_employees_by_department(data, top_n)` that processes this dataset and returns a dictionary. The keys of this dictionary should be department names, and the values should be lists of tuples. Each tuple should contain the name and salary of one of the top `top_n` highest-paid employees in that department, sorted by salary in descending order.

**Input:**
- `data`: A list of dictionaries, where each dictionary represents an employee.
- `top_n`: An integer representing the number of top-paid employees to retrieve per department.

**Output:**
- A dictionary where each key is a department name and each value is a list of tuples. Each tuple contains the name and salary of an employee, sorted by salary in descending order.

**Sample Input:**
```python
employees = [
    {"name": "Alice", "department": "Engineering", "salary": 90000, "hire_date": "2019-06-15"},
    {"name": "Bob", "department": "Marketing", "salary": 85000, "hire_date": "2018-03-20"},
    {"name": "Charlie", "department": "Engineering", "salary": 120000, "hire_date": "2017-11-02"},
    {"name": "David", "department": "Engineering", "salary": 105000, "hire_date": "2016-07-14"},
    {"name": "Eve", "department": "Marketing", "salary": 95000, "hire_date": "2019-09-27"},
    {"name": "Frank", "department": "HR", "salary": 75000, "hire_date": "2020-02-10"},
    {"name": "Grace", "department": "Engineering", "salary": 115000, "hire_date": "2018-08-31"}
]
top_n = 3
```

**Sample Output:**
```python
{
    'Engineering': [('Grace', 115000), ('Charlie', 120000), ('David', 105000)],
    'Marketing': [('Eve', 95000), ('Bob', 85000)],
    'HR': [('Frank', 75000)]
}
```

```python
def get_top_paid_employees_by_department(data, top_n):
    """
    Returns a dictionary with department names as keys and lists of tuples as values.
    Each tuple contains the name and salary of one of the top_n highest-paid employees in that department.
    
    :param data: List of dictionaries, each representing an employee.
    :param top_n: Integer, number of top-paid employees to retrieve per department.
    :return: Dictionary with department names as keys and lists of (name, salary) tuples as values.
    """
    from collections import defaultdict

    department_employees = defaultdict(list)
    for employee in data:
        department_employees[employee['department']].append((employee['name'], employee['salary']))

    top_paid_employees = {}
    for department, employees in department_employees.items():
        sorted_employees = sorted(employees, key=lambda x: x[1], reverse=True)
        top_paid_employees[department] = sorted_employees[:top_n]

    return top_paid_employees
```<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `Rectangle` class in Python that models a rectangle in 2D space. The class should support the following operations:

- **Initialization**: The constructor `__init__` should accept two parameters, `width` and `height`, both of which must be positive numbers. If either parameter is non-positive, the constructor should raise a `ValueError` with an appropriate message.
- **Area Calculation**: Implement a method `get_area` that returns the area of the rectangle.
- **Perimeter Calculation**: Implement a method `get_perimeter` that returns the perimeter of the rectangle.
- **Scaling**: Implement a method `scale` that takes a scaling factor as an argument and scales both the width and height of the rectangle by this factor. The scaling factor must be a positive number; otherwise, a `ValueError` should be raised.
- **String Representation**: Implement a method `__str__` that returns a string representation of the rectangle in the format `"Rectangle(width=<width>, height=<height>)"`.

**Sample Input/Output**:
- Input: `rect = Rectangle(3, 4)`
- Output: `rect.get_area()` should return `12`
- Output: `rect.get_perimeter()` should return `14`
- Output: `rect.scale(2)` should change `rect` to `Rectangle(width=6, height=8)`
- Output: `str(rect)` should return `"Rectangle(width=6, height=8)"`

```python
class Rectangle:
    def __init__(self, width, height):
        """
        Initializes a new Rectangle instance with the given width and height.
        
        :param width: The width of the rectangle (must be positive).
        :param height: The height of the rectangle (must be positive).
        :raises ValueError: If width or height is not positive.
        """
        if width <= 0:
            raise ValueError("Width must be a positive number")
        if height <= 0:
            raise ValueError("Height must be a positive number")
        self.width = width
        self.height = height

    def get_area(self):
        """
        Calculates the area of the rectangle.
        
        :return: The area of the rectangle.
        """
        return self.width * self.height

    def get_perimeter(self):
        """
        Calculates the perimeter of the rectangle.
        
        :return: The perimeter of the rectangle.
        """
        return 2 * (self.width + self.height)

    def scale(self, factor):
        """
        Scales the rectangle by the given factor.
        
        :param factor: The scaling factor (must be positive).
        :raises ValueError: If the scaling factor is not positive.
        """
        if factor <= 0:
            raise ValueError("Scaling factor must be a positive number")
        self.width *= factor
        self.height *= factor

    def __str__(self):
        """
        Returns a string representation of the rectangle.
        
        :return: A string in the format "Rectangle(width=<width>, height=<height>)".
        """
        return f"Rectangle(width={self.width}, height={self.height})"
```<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `BankAccount` class in Python that supports basic operations such as deposit, withdraw, and check balance. The class should handle errors to prevent the account balance from going negative. The `BankAccount` class should have the following attributes: `account_number`, `account_holder`, and `balance`. Initialize these attributes in the constructor with default values of `None`, `None`, and `0`, respectively. Implement methods for depositing and withdrawing money, and a method to check the current balance.

**Input:**
- The `BankAccount` class will be instantiated with `account_number`, `account_holder`, and `balance`.
- The `deposit` method will be called with a positive integer `amount`.
- The `withdraw` method will be called with a positive integer `amount`.

**Output:**
- The `deposit` method should print the deposited amount and the new balance.
- The `withdraw` method should print the withdrawn amount and the new balance, or raise a `ValueError` if the withdrawal amount exceeds the balance.
- The `check_balance` method should print and return the current balance.

**Sample Input/Output:**
```python
account = BankAccount('123456789', 'John Doe', 100)
account.deposit(50)  # Output: Deposited $50. New balance is $150.
account.withdraw(30) # Output: Withdrew $30. New balance is $120.
account.check_balance() # Output: Current balance is $120.
```

```python
class BankAccount:
    def __init__(self, account_number=None, account_holder=None, balance=0):
        """
        Initialize a new bank account with the given account number, account holder, and balance.
        
        :param account_number: The account number of the bank account.
        :param account_holder: The name of the account holder.
        :param balance: The initial balance of the account.
        """
        self.account_number = account_number
        self.account_holder = account_holder
        self.balance = balance

    def deposit(self, amount):
        """
        Deposit a specified amount into the bank account.
        
        :param amount: The amount to deposit.
        :raises ValueError: If the deposit amount is negative.
        """
        if amount < 0:
            raise ValueError("Deposit amount must be positive.")
        self.balance += amount
        print(f"Deposited ${amount}. New balance is ${self.balance}.")

    def withdraw(self, amount):
        """
        Withdraw a specified amount from the bank account.
        
        :param amount: The amount to withdraw.
        :raises ValueError: If the withdrawal amount is negative or exceeds the current balance.
        """
        if amount < 0:
            raise ValueError("Withdrawal amount must be positive.")
        if amount > self.balance:
            raise ValueError("Insufficient funds for withdrawal.")
        self.balance -= amount
        print(f"Withdrew ${amount}. New balance is ${self.balance}.")

    def check_balance(self):
        """
        Check and return the current balance of the bank account.
        
        :return: The current balance.
        """
        print(f"Current balance is ${self.balance}.")
        return self.balance
```<#sep#>Implement a Python program to simulate a simple game of Rock-Paper-Scissors. The program should interact with the user by prompting for their choice, randomly generate a choice for the computer, and then determine the winner based on the standard rules of the game. The program must handle invalid inputs gracefully by informing the user of the mistake.

**Input:**
- A single line containing the user's choice: either "rock", "paper", or "scissors". The input is case-insensitive and may contain leading or trailing spaces.

**Output:**
- Print the user's choice.
- Print the computer's randomly selected choice.
- Print the result of the game: "You win!", "Computer wins!", "It's a tie!", or "Invalid input. Please enter rock, paper, or scissors."

**Sample Input 1:**
```
rock
```

**Sample Output 1:**
```
Your choice: rock
Computer's choice: paper
Computer wins!
```

**Sample Input 2:**
```
PAPER
```

**Sample Output 2:**
```
Your choice: paper
Computer's choice: rock
You win!
```

**Sample Input 3:**
```
scissors
```

**Sample Output 3:**
```
Your choice: scissors
Computer's choice: scissors
It's a tie!
```

**Sample Input 4:**
```
lizard
```

**Sample Output 4:**
```
Your choice: lizard
Computer's choice: rock
Invalid input. Please enter rock, paper, or scissors.
```

```python
import random

def get_computer_choice():
    """
    Randomly selects and returns the computer's choice from 'rock', 'paper', or 'scissors'.
    """
    choices = ['rock', 'paper', 'scissors']
    return random.choice(choices)

def get_user_choice():
    """
    Prompts the user to enter their choice and returns it in lowercase, stripped of leading/trailing spaces.
    """
    user_input = input("Enter your choice (rock, paper, scissors): ").strip().lower()
    return user_input

def determine_winner(user_choice, computer_choice):
    """
    Determines the winner of the Rock-Paper-Scissors game based on the user's and computer's choices.
    Returns the result as a string.
    """
    if user_choice == computer_choice:
        return "It's a tie!"
    elif (user_choice == 'rock' and computer_choice == 'scissors') or \
         (user_choice == 'scissors' and computer_choice == 'paper') or \
         (user_choice == 'paper' and computer_choice == 'rock'):
        return "You win!"
    elif user_choice not in ['rock', 'paper', 'scissors']:
        return "Invalid input. Please enter rock, paper, or scissors."
    else:
        return "Computer wins!"

def play_game():
    """
    Orchestrates the Rock-Paper-Scissors game by getting user and computer choices, determining the winner, and displaying the results.
    """
    print("Welcome to Rock-Paper-Scissors!")
    user_choice = get_user_choice()
    computer_choice = get_computer_choice()
    
    print(f"Your choice: {user_choice}")
    print(f"Computer's choice: {computer_choice}")
    
    result = determine_winner(user_choice, computer_choice)
    print(result)

# Play the game
play_game()
```<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `BookstoreInventory` class to manage a bookstore's inventory. The class should support the following operations:

1. **Add a Book**: Add a book to the inventory. Each book is represented by its `title`, `author`, and `price`.
2. **Remove a Book**: Remove a book from the inventory by its `title`.
3. **List Books**: Return a list of all books currently in the inventory.

Your implementation should include a `Book` class to represent individual books. Each `Book` object should have attributes `title`, `author`, and `price`.

**Input:**
- A series of commands to manipulate the inventory. Each command is on a new line.
- Commands can be:
  - `ADD <title> <author> <price>`: Add a book with the given title, author, and price.
  - `REMOVE <title>`: Remove the book with the given title.
  - `LIST`: List all books in the inventory.

**Output:**
- For each `LIST` command, output the list of books in the format `Book(title='<title>', author='<author>', price=<price>)`, one book per line.

**Sample Input:**
```
ADD 1984 GeorgeOrwell 9.99
ADD ToKillAMockingbird HarperLee 8.99
LIST
REMOVE 1984
LIST
```

**Sample Output:**
```
Book(title='1984', author='GeorgeOrwell', price=9.99)
Book(title='ToKillAMockingbird', author='HarperLee', price=8.99)

Book(title='ToKillAMockingbird', author='HarperLee', price=8.99)
```

```python
class Book:
    def __init__(self, title, author, price):
        self.title = title
        self.author = author
        self.price = price

    def __repr__(self):
        return f"Book(title='{self.title}', author='{self.author}', price={self.price})"

class BookstoreInventory:
    def __init__(self):
        self.books = []

    def add_book(self, book):
        """Add a book to the inventory."""
        self.books.append(book)

    def remove_book(self, title):
        """Remove a book from the inventory by its title."""
        self.books = [book for book in self.books if book.title != title]

    def list_books(self):
        """Return a list of all books in the inventory."""
        return self.books

# Demonstration of using the BookstoreInventory class
if __name__ == "__main__":
    inventory = BookstoreInventory()
    
    commands = [
        "ADD 1984 GeorgeOrwell 9.99",
        "ADD ToKillAMockingbird HarperLee 8.99",
        "LIST",
        "REMOVE 1984",
        "LIST"
    ]
    
    for command in commands:
        parts = command.split()
        if parts[0] == "ADD":
            title, author, price = parts[1], parts[2], float(parts[3])
            book = Book(title, author, price)
            inventory.add_book(book)
        elif parts[0] == "REMOVE":
            title = parts[1]
            inventory.remove_book(title)
        elif parts[0] == "LIST":
            for book in inventory.list_books():
                print(book)
            print()
